--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:** The champion is remarkably simple: 1-state GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3), score 3582.05. This makes perfect sense - robots just head toward the blue walls and stay there. The gap between #1 (3582) and #2 (6143) is huge, confirming GoToColor blue alone is excellent.

**Why it works:** Blue walls border the target strip. GoToColor makes robots move toward blue, putting them in the target region. The black floor detection isn't needed - just head to blue walls.

**Strategy for Round 3:**
1. **Survival**: Champion verbatim (1-state GoToColor blue)
2. **Exploitation**: Variants of the champion with slight modifications:
   - Add a transition to handle edge cases (when already on black floor, stay or continue)
   - Try different LED colors (doesn't affect behavior)
   - 2-state with GoToColor blue primary + small correction behavior
3. **Exploration**: 
   - Try stopping when on black floor (to stay in target zone)
   - Try flocking toward blue
   - 2-state: GoToColor blue, then stop when on black
   - GoToColor blue + neighbor-based clustering to aggregate within target

The key question: can we do better than pure GoToColor blue? Perhaps by stopping once in the target (black floor) to avoid wandering back out.

**Promising 2-state design:**
- State 0: GoToColor blue → if floor is black (in strip), transition to State 1
- State 1: Stop (or slow explore) → if floor is gray (left target), transition to State 0

**Exploitation tweaks of #2 (6143):** The 2-state GoToColor+explore was second best. Try with better transition probabilities.

Let me also try:
- GoToColor blue with stop-on-black
- GoToColor blue with attraction when many neighbors nearby
- Pure stop (shouldn't work but baseline)

## Reasoning

**Key findings:**
- The champion (3509.55) is simply 1-state GoToColor blue. Two copies tied for 1st/2nd with different LED colors.
- The simple 1-state GoToColor blue is very hard to beat. Any additional complexity seems to hurt.
- 2-state machines that transition away from GoToColor blue when on black floor (stopping or exploring) score 5000-11000 - worse.
- The best 2-state variant (#3, score 5262) has GoToColor blue → explore when on black OR gray, but still worse than pure GoToColor.

**Why pure GoToColor blue wins:** Robots continuously move toward blue walls, keeping them in the target strip. Any transition away from this behavior (stopping, exploring) causes them to drift back.

**Strategy for Round 4:**
1. Survive with champion (pure GoToColor blue)
2. Try GoToColor blue with very low probability transitions (nearly pure)
3. Try different clr values to see if another color interpretation helps
4. Try GoToColor blue + small neighbor-based staying behavior
5. Try if stopping only when seeing many neighbors (already clustered) helps
6. The 2-state with explore (rank 3-4) had transitions on black floor with low probability (0.15-0.2) - these were better than high prob transitions. Try even lower probabilities.
7. Try GoToColor with clr=6 (cyan - could blue walls appear cyan?)
8. Try 2-state: GoToColor blue primary, with a very rare random transition back

The main insight: the 2-state machines that transition when on BLACK floor with p=0.15-0.2 to explore scored ~5262-5368. These still beat stop-based approaches. But all are worse than pure GoToColor.

Let me focus: the champion is nearly optimal. I should try small variations and also genuinely different approaches:
- Can we use clr=4 (yellow)? No - target is blue walls
- Try Spiral toward blue?
- The issue might be that some robots get stuck at the blue wall bouncing. 

For exploration: try GoToColor blue combined with staying behavior only when MANY neighbors are present (already in group), otherwise keep going to blue.

**Note on errors:** Previous errors were about missing destination indices. I need to be careful: `--n<I>x<K> D` requires D, and `--n<I>` requires the count. Let me be very precise.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 4:**

The new champion (2994.17) is:
- State 0: GoToColor blue → if black floor (p=0.9) go to state 1, OR if many neighbors (≥6, steep sigmoid) go to state 1
- State 1: Attraction (att=2.5) → if gray floor (p=0.9) back to state 0, OR if few neighbors (<3, steep) back to state 0

This is elegant: **GoToColor blue to reach target, then Attraction to aggregate with others already there**. The attraction keeps robots clustered in the target zone.

**Analysis:**
- Champion: GoToColor blue → (black floor OR many neighbors) → Attraction → (gray floor OR few neighbors) → back
- #2 (3531): GoToColor blue → (many neighbors) → FlockCenter → (few neighbors) → back (similar logic)
- The gap between #1 (2994) and #2 (3531) is meaningful but not huge

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Tweak att gain (try 2.0, 3.0, 3.5)
2. Tweak neighbor thresholds (try 5, 7 instead of 6)
3. Tweak sigmoid steepness
4. Tweak transition probabilities (0.85, 0.95)
5. Try FlockCenter instead of Attraction in state 1
6. Try adding a 3rd state

**Exploration ideas:**
1. GoToColor blue → Stop (when on black) → GoToColor (when gray)
2. GoToColor blue → FlockCenter → back
3. 3-state: GoToColor → Attraction → Stop
4. Try different attraction gains combined with floor color only (no neighbor condition)

The champion topology: GoToColor(blue) with two exit conditions (floor black + many neighbors) → Attraction with two exit conditions (floor gray + few neighbors).

Let me focus on exploitation of this topology:

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 5:**

The champion (2994.17) remains the same 2-state GoToColor-blue → Attraction machine. All top machines in round 5 are very close variants (3059-3180 range), suggesting we're near a local optimum for this topology.

**What's working:**
- State 0: GoToColor blue with transitions on: black floor (p=0.9) AND many neighbors above threshold (p0x1=6, w=12)
- State 1: Attraction (att≈2-3) with transitions on: gray floor (p=0.9) AND few neighbors below threshold (p1x1=2-3, w=8-10)

**Fine-tuning observations:**
- att=2.5 vs att=2.0 vs att=3.0: all very close (~3059-3068), slight edge to att=2.5
- w0x1=12 seems good; 15 gives same score
- p1x1 threshold: 2 with w=10 slightly better than 3 with w=8
- 3-state versions are much worse (8000+)
- FlockCenter worse than Attraction

**Strategy for Round 6:**
1. Survive with champion
2. Fine-tune: try att=1.5, att=1.8, att=2.2
3. Fine-tune: try neighbor thresholds 4 and 5 in state 0
4. Fine-tune: try p0x0 probabilities 0.92, 0.95
5. Fine-tune: try w values 10, 14 for state 0 neighbor condition
6. Try: only floor-color transitions (no neighbor), simpler
7. Try: floor color only in state 0, neighbor only in state 1
8. Try: 2-state with GoToColor → Stop (not Attraction)
9. Try: adding a small repulsion component when too many neighbors

Key insight: the neighbor condition in state 0 (transition when many neighbors detected) + floor color seem complementary. Let me explore variations more carefully.

Also note: `--n1x1 0 --c1x1 4 --p1x1 2 --w1x1 10` (threshold=2, steep=10) gave best score 3059. Let me try threshold=1 and threshold=4.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 7:**

The champion (2972.02) is the same core machine: GoToColor(blue) → [black floor p=0.9 OR neighbors≥6 w=12] → Attraction(2.5) → [gray floor p=0.9 OR neighbors<3 w=8] → back.

Notable: the best result this round (#1) is the **champion itself** re-evaluated, and #2 (3046) uses p=0.92 with threshold=3, w=9. The original champion params (p=0.9, threshold=3, w=8) keep winning.

**What we've learned:**
- The topology is locked in: 2-state GoToColor blue + Attraction
- Best: p=0.9, neighbor threshold=3, w=8 for state 1 exit; threshold=6, w=12 for state 0 exit
- att=2.5 is best
- Adding extra transitions hurts
- 3-state versions hurt significantly

**Convergence situation:** We're at a very tight local optimum. The scores range 2972-3838, very clustered around the champion. I need to:
1. Keep champion
2. Try very fine parameter variations around the sweet spot
3. Try some genuinely different approaches to escape

**Fine-tuning to try:**
- p=0.89, 0.91 (around 0.9)
- threshold=6, w=11, 13 (around 12) for state 0
- threshold=3, w=7, 9 (around 8) for state 1
- att=2.4, 2.6

**New explorations:**
- Try GoToColor blue + FlockCenter (different from Attraction)
- Try the topology with only floor color conditions (no neighbor)
- Try GoToColor blue + GoToColor blue (same color, different LED, to see if re-evaluation helps)
- Try att=1.5 combined with different thresholds
- Try a 4-state machine with more complex behavior

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 8:**

The champion (2972.02) still holds. Many variants scored identically at 3063.31 - this suggests stochastic evaluation is causing noise, and we're very tightly converged around this local optimum. The topology is fixed: GoToColor(blue) + Attraction.

**What we've confirmed:**
- p=0.9 is optimal (p=0.89, 0.91, 0.92 all slightly worse)
- w0x1=12 is optimal (11, 13 give same score 3063, but 12 gave champion's 2972)
- att=2.5 is optimal (2.4, 2.6 give same score - stochastic noise)
- w1x1=8 is optimal (7, 9 slightly worse)
- threshold=6 for state 0 is good (5 worse, 7 slightly worse)
- threshold=3 for state 1 is good
- FlockCenter is worse than Attraction

**The champion re-evaluated at 3063 this round vs 2972 before - this is just stochastic noise.** The machine is good but stochastic.

**Strategy for Round 9 (2 rounds left):**
I need to both preserve the champion AND try to find something genuinely better. The current approach is very tightly tuned. Let me try:
1. Champion verbatim (survival)
2. A few more micro-variations of champion
3. **Genuinely different topologies** that might escape the local optimum:
   - 3-state: GoToColor → Attraction → Stop (when on black and many neighbors)
   - GoToColor with different color (yellow=4, cyan=6) - maybe robots can see wall colors differently
   - Try asymmetric probabilities (different p for the two transitions in each state)
   - Try GoToColor blue with only floor condition (no neighbor)
   - Try swapping: Attraction first, then GoToColor when few neighbors

**Key insight I haven't tried:** What if the attraction state uses a DIFFERENT transition condition? Currently it exits on gray floor OR few neighbors. What if it exits only on gray floor (high confidence)?

Also: I notice the scores cluster at 3063.31 for many variants - this is suspicious, suggesting it's the same simulation seed result. The champion's 2972 was a lucky seed. I should try submitting the same machine multiple times to get more samples.

Let me be strategic: submit the champion + close variants + some genuine exploration:

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 9:**

New champion (2869.78) uses a crucial change: **state 1 exit condition changed from gray floor to BLACK floor with p=0.1**. This is counterintuitive but works:
- State 0: GoToColor blue → if black floor (p=0.9) OR many neighbors (≥6, w=12) → go to Attraction
- State 1: Attraction → if **black** floor (p=0.1, rare) OR few neighbors (<3, w=8) → back to GoToColor

The black floor exit from Attraction (p=0.1) means: while doing attraction in the target zone, with 10% probability per step when on black floor, go back to GoToColor blue. This creates a subtle "re-centering" behavior - robots occasionally re-seek the blue wall even while attracting.

**Observations:**
- Gray floor exit (C=1, p=0.9) was score 2890 - good but not as good
- Black floor exit (C=0, p=0.1) was score 2869 - best!
- White floor exit (C=2) never tried in state 1 - could be interesting (but no white floor here)
- Threshold=3, w=8 confirmed best for neighbor exit
- The 3-state machines are much worse
- Only floor color + neighbor count transitions in the 2-state topology work

**What to try in Round 10 (final round):**
1. Champion verbatim
2. Fine-tune p0x0 (black floor prob in state 1): try 0.05, 0.08, 0.12, 0.15
3. Try combining both black floor exit AND neighbor exit in state 1 with different params
4. Try att values around 2.5 with the new champion topology
5. Try w1x1 around 8 with new topology
6. Try threshold=4 in state 1 with new topology
7. Try p0x0 (state 0 black floor transition) variations around 0.9

The key insight: C=0 (black floor) with low probability in state 1 is better than C=1 (gray floor) with high probability. This makes sense because:
- Gray floor exit means leaving attraction as soon as you leave the target - too reactive
- Black floor exit with low prob means staying in attraction most of the time even in target, but occasionally refreshing the GoToColor behavior

Let me focus on this new champion topology and tune it carefully.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
